<div><tool-call id="toolu_015NLfVJBLJgwhqFVLuaBUtb"><script type="application/json">{"version":2,"name":"read","input":{"file_path":"person-grid-walkthrough.qmd"},"caption":"person-grid-walkthrough.qmd","captionLink":"person-grid-walkthrough.qmd"}</script></tool-call></div>

<div><tool-result call-id="toolu_015NLfVJBLJgwhqFVLuaBUtb"><script type="application/json">{"version":1,"status":"success","result":{"type":"text","value":"[File: 14.2KB, 373 lines, showing lines 1-373]\n1\t---\n2\ttitle: \"`person-grid.R`: a parametr...[truncated, total 15918 chars]"},"resultPreview":{"type":"code","value":"[File: 14.2KB, 373 lines, showing lines 1-373]\n---\ntitle: \"`person-grid.R`: a parametrized pictogram generator\"\nsubtitle: \"Walkthrough: how the script works, and how to use it in Quarto\"\nformat:\n  html:\n    code-fold: false\n    toc: true\n---\n\n```{r}\n#| label: setup\n#| include: false\nknitr::opts_chunk$set(fig.align = \"center\")\n```\n\n## Aims\n\n`person-grid.R` draws \"restroom-sign style\" person icons — plain polygons,\nnot an icon font or image file — and arranges any number of them into a\ngrid. It's the base building block behind several other figures in this\nbook: a single icon reused by `testing-sequence.R` (a person at a computer)\nand by `peer-review-workflow.R` (author/editor/reviewer roles), plus two\nfigures used directly:\n\n- a **diversity sample** figure — a grid of icons varying in silhouette,\n  age, disability, and skin tone, to make \"any group of people will vary\"\n  concrete rather than abstract;\n- an **effect grid** figure — the same grid mechanism, but with each icon's\n  fill colour driven by a numeric value (e.g. an effect size or score) on a\n  diverging blue–white–orange scale, to show \"people vary in how they\n  respond\" to some intervention or condition.\n\nThis document explains how the script is built, then how to call it from a\nQuarto chunk.\n\n## Prerequisites / assumptions\n\n- **Packages**: `ggplot2` only (loaded by the script itself). No icon\n  fonts or external image assets — every icon is drawn from coordinates,\n  so it renders identically wherever Quarto builds the document.\n- **Sourcing**: standalone — nothing else needs to be sourced first. (It\n  is, however, a dependency *of* `testing-sequence.R` and\n  `peer-review-workflow.R` — see their own walkthroughs.)\n- **Reader background**: basic familiarity with `ggplot2` (`geom_polygon()`,\n  `aes()`, `theme_void()`) is assumed; no statistics background needed.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: source-script\nsource(\"person-grid.R\")\n```\n\n## How it works: the process\n\nEach icon is just a handful of flat shapes (head, torso, legs or\nwheelchair, optional cane) assembled as polygons in a local coordinate\nframe, then offset into a grid position. Colour and demographic\ncomposition are decided by sampling from user-specified proportions. The\npipeline for building the whole grid looks like this:\n\n```{mermaid}\nflowchart LR\n    A[\"person_parts()\\n(one icon's polygons)\"] --> E[\"assemble + offset\\ninto grid positions\"]\n    B[\"sample_counts()\\n(silhouette / age / disability mix)\"] --> E\n    C[\"diverging_colors() or\\nsampled skin tones\\n(fill colour)\"] --> E\n    D[\"choose_ncol()\\n(grid shape)\"] --> E\n    E --> F[\"ggplot() + geom_polygon()\\n+ scale_fill_identity()\"]\n```\n\nIn short: **geometry** (what shape is each icon) is decided independently\nfrom **composition** (how many of each kind) and **colour** (what fill\neach gets), and all three are combined only at the very end when the\npolygon data frame is built and handed to `ggplot()`.\n\n## Element-by-element walkthrough\n\n### Low-level geometry: `circle_pts()` and `person_parts()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-circle-pts\n#| eval: false\ncircle_pts \u003c- function(cx, cy, r, n = 24) {\n  t \u003c- seq(0, 2 * pi, length.out = n + 1)[-(n + 1)]\n  data.frame(x = cx + r * cos(t), y = cy + r * sin(t))\n}\n```\n\n`circle_pts()` is a tiny helper that turns a centre + radius into `n`\npoints around a circle (used for the head, and for the wheelchair wheel).\nIt's deliberately generic — nothing about it is specific to drawing people.\n\n`person_parts(kind, age, disabled, scale, ox, oy)` is where one icon's\nshape is actually defined. It builds a **named list** of polygons (head,\ntorso, legs, etc.) in a fixed local coordinate frame (head centred at\n`x = 0, y = 1.62`), then at the very end:\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-offset-logic\n#| eval: false\nout \u003c- lapply(names(parts), function(nm) {\n  p \u003c- parts[[nm]]\n  data.frame(part = nm, x = ox + scale * p$x, y = oy + scale * p$y)\n})\ndo.call(rbind, out)\n```\n\nscales and offsets every part by `(ox, oy)` — this is *the* mechanism that\nlets the same local shape be placed anywhere in a grid (or, in\n`testing-sequence.R`, next to a monitor icon) just by changing `ox`/`oy`.\n\nThree design choices worth knowing about, because they explain shapes you\nmight otherwise find surprising in the rendered figure:\n\n- `kind` controls the leg silhouette: `\"A\"` (trouser-coded, two separate leg\n  bars), `\"B\"` (skirt-coded, one flared polygon), or `\"N\"` — literally half\n  of each — an inclusive, non-binary-coded silhouette rather than a third\n  stereotyped shape.\n- `disabled = TRUE` swaps the torso/legs for a single wheelchair wheel plus\n  a footrest peg, redesigned (per a code comment in the script) as one big\n  unbroken shape rather than several small ones, because small shapes stop\n  reading clearly once icons are shrunk down in a large grid.\n- `age = \"elder\"` adds a cane polygon; `age = \"child\"` doesn't change the\n  shape at all — it's handled later, as an overall `scale` multiplier in\n  `person_grid()`, not inside `person_parts()`.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-person-parts\n#| fig-cap: \"A single adult icon, drawn directly from person_parts()\"\none_person \u003c- person_parts(\"A\", \"adult\", disabled = FALSE)\nggplot(one_person, aes(x, y, group = part)) +\n  geom_polygon(fill = \"#3B6EA5\") +\n  coord_equal() +\n  theme_void()\n```\n\n### Composition sampling: `sample_counts()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-sample-counts\n#| eval: false\nsample_counts \u003c- function(n, props, seed) {\n  stopifnot(\n    \"`props` must have non-negative values, with at least one positive\" =\n      all(props >= 0) && sum(props) > 0\n  )\n  props \u003c- props / sum(props)\n  set.seed(seed)\n  raw \u003c- props * n\n  counts \u003c- floor(raw)\n  remainder \u003c- n - sum(counts)\n  if (remainder > 0) {\n    order \u003c- order(-(raw - counts))\n    for (i in seq_len(remainder)) {\n      idx \u003c- order[((i - 1) %% length(order)) + 1]\n      counts[idx] \u003c- counts[idx] + 1\n    }\n  }\n  sample(rep(names(props), counts))\n}\n```\n\nGiven target proportions (e.g. `c(adult = 0.70, child = 0.15, elder =\n0.15)`) and a grid size `n`, this turns proportions into **exact integer\ncounts** using the largest-remainder method (the same allocation method\nused for apportioning seats to parties in some electoral systems): take the\nfloor of each `proportion * n`, then hand out the few leftover icons to\nwhichever categories had the largest fractional remainder. This hits the\nrequested mix as closely as integers allow, rather than independently\nsampling each icon (which could, by chance, give you 0 children out of 60\neven at `child = 0.15`). The final `sample()` call shuffles the result so\nicons of the same kind aren't clumped together in the grid.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-sample-counts\nsample_counts(10, c(adult = 0.7, child = 0.15, elder = 0.15), seed = 1)\n```\n\nNote the `stopifnot` requires *at least one* positive proportion, but zero\nis allowed for the others — so `disabled_prop = 0` (the default in some\ncontexts) is fine: it just means that category gets 0 of the `n` icons, not\nan error.\n\n### Colour: `diverging_colors()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-diverging-colors\n#| eval: false\ndiverging_colors \u003c- function(value, neg = \"#0072B2\", mid = \"#F2F1EC\", pos = \"#E69F00\") {\n  span \u003c- max(abs(value), 1e-9)\n  tt \u003c- value / span                     # -1 .. 1, 0 = null\n  ramp_lo \u003c- grDevices::colorRamp(c(neg, mid))\n  ramp_hi \u003c- grDevices::colorRamp(c(mid, pos))\n  to_hex \u003c- function(m) grDevices::rgb(m[, 1], m[, 2], m[, 3], maxColorValue = 255)\n  vapply(tt, function(t) {\n    if (t \u003c= 0) to_hex(ramp_lo(t + 1))\n    else        to_hex(ramp_hi(t))\n  }, character(1))\n}\n```\n\nThis is the colour engine behind `person_grid()`'s `mode = \"value\"` —\ne.g. colouring icons by an effect size or score. It's a **symmetric,\ndiverging** scale: `0` is always treated as the reference/\"null\" point\n(light, near-white), regardless of where your data happen to sit, and the\ntwo endpoints (default blue/orange — the Okabe–Ito colour-blind-safe\npalette, not a red/green or red/blue scale) are reached only at\n`max(abs(value))`. That symmetry matters pedagogically: a `value` vector\nof `c(-1, 3)` will *not* make `-1` look strongly negative, because `3` sets\nthe scale — worth knowing if your values aren't already roughly symmetric\naround zero.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-diverging-colors\n#| fig-cap: \"The diverging scale at a few sample values\"\nvals \u003c- c(-2, -1, 0, 1, 2)\ndata.frame(value = vals, fill = diverging_colors(vals)) |>\n  ggplot(aes(x = value, y = 1, fill = fill)) +\n  geom_tile(width = 0.9, height = 0.9) +\n  scale_fill_identity() +\n  theme_void()\n```\n\n### Automatic grid shape: `choose_ncol()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-choose-ncol\n#| eval: false\nchoose_ncol \u003c- function(n, dx, dy, aspect) {\n  ncols \u003c- seq_len(n)\n  nrows \u003c- ceiling(n / ncols)\n  grid_ratio \u003c- (ncols * dx) / (nrows * dy)\n  ncols[which.min(abs(log(grid_ratio / aspect)))]\n}\n```\n\nGiven `n` icons and a target width:height `aspect` ratio (default 16:9),\nthis brute-forces every possible number of columns from 1 to `n` and picks\nwhichever makes the resulting grid's bounding box closest to that aspect\nratio — so the figure fills a slide with minimal leftover margin, without\nyou having to hand-pick `ncol` for every `n`. It compares ratios on a *log*\nscale, so \"twice too wide\" and \"twice too tall\" count as equally bad,\nrather than the search being biased toward wide grids.\n\n### Putting it together: `person_grid()`\n\nThis is the function you actually call. It:\n\n1. Resolves `ncol` (via `choose_ncol()` if left as `\"auto\"`).\n2. Samples silhouette/age/disability composition via `sample_counts()`.\n3. Resolves fill colour — either sampled skin tones (`mode = \"diversity\"`)\n   or `diverging_colors(value)` (`mode = \"value\"`).\n4. Loops over `1:n`, computing each icon's grid row/column (and hence\n   `ox`/`oy`), calling `person_parts()` for its shape (children are drawn\n   at `scale = 0.65`), and stamping on that icon's fill colour.\n5. Row-binds every icon's polygons into one data frame and builds a single\n   `ggplot()` call — one `geom_polygon()` for the whole grid, not `n`\n   separate layers, grouped by a per-icon `poly_id`.\n6. Also returns `meta` (a tidy one-row-per-icon summary) and `alt_text` (a\n   plain-language description of the composition, generated from the same\n   sampling draws used to build the icons) — handy for `fig-alt`.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-person-grid-diversity\n#| fig-cap: \"person_grid() in diversity mode\"\n#| fig-width: 7\n#| fig-height: 3\ndiversity_demo \u003c- person_grid(n = 24, seed = 11)\ndiversity_demo$plot\n```\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-person-grid-value\n#| fig-cap: \"person_grid() in value mode, coloured by a simulated effect size\"\n#| fig-width: 7\n#| fig-height: 3\nset.seed(2716)\nvalue_demo \u003c- person_grid(n = 24, mode = \"value\", value = rnorm(24, mean = 0, sd = 1.2))\nvalue_demo$plot\n```\n\n## Quarto usage / integration\n\nMinimal setup-chunk line:\n\n```r\nsource(\"person-grid.R\")\n```\n\nReal usage from `doing-being-science.qmd` (the diversity-sample slide):\n\n````{verbatim}\n```{r}\n#| label: fig-diversity-sample\n#| fig-cap: \"The people in any group will vary in many ways: just look around you\"\n#| fig-alt: !expr diversity_grid$alt_text\n#| fig-width: 8\n#| fig-height: 4.5\n\ndiversity_grid \u003c- person_grid(n = 125, seed = 11)\ndiversity_grid$plot\n```\n````\n\nAnd the value-mode / effect-size variant:\n\n````{verbatim}\n```{r}\n#| label: fig-effect-grid-demo\n#| fig-cap: \"People vary and how conditions or interventions affect us will vary: from strongly negative (dark blue) to null (near-white) to strongly positive (dark red) impacts\"\n#| fig-alt: !expr effect_grid$alt_text\n#| fig-width: 8\n#| fig-height: 4.5\n\nset.seed(2716)\neffect_grid \u003c- person_grid(n = 60, mode = \"value\", value = rnorm(60, mean = 0, sd = 1.2))\neffect_grid$plot\n```\n````\n\nTwo things worth keeping in mind when reusing this pattern:\n\n- `fig-alt` is set via `!expr` to pull the *already-computed*\n  `alt_text` field out of the object returned by `person_grid()` — so the\n  alt text always matches what was actually sampled, rather than being\n  hand-written and liable to drift out of sync.\n- Because `coord_equal(expand = FALSE)` is used internally, the figure's\n  panel is exactly the icons' bounding box with no extra margin — set\n  `fig-width`/`fig-height` to roughly match the grid's aspect ratio so\n  Reveal/HTML doesn't stretch it oddly.\n\n### Parameter quick reference\n\n| Parameter | Default | Meaning |\n|---|---|---|\n| `n` | `30` | Number of icons. |\n| `ncol` | `\"auto\"` | Icons per row; `\"auto\"` picks the best fit to `aspect`. |\n| `aspect` | `16/9` | Target width:height ratio used only when `ncol = \"auto\"`. |\n| `comp_kind` | `c(A=.45, B=.45, N=.10)` | Silhouette mix (trouser / skirt / half-half). |\n| `comp_age` | `c(adult=.70, child=.15, elder=.15)` | Age-presentation mix. |\n| `disabled_prop` | `0.12` | Proportion drawn with the wheelchair silhouette. |\n| `mode` | `\"diversity\"` | `\"diversity\"` (sampled skin tone) or `\"value\"` (diverging fill from `value`). |\n| `skin_tones` | 6 hex colours | Palette sampled from when `mode = \"diversity\"`. |\n| `value` | `NULL` | Numeric vector, length `n`; required when `mode = \"value\"`. |\n| `seed` | `1` | Reproducibility seed for every sampling step. |\n| `dx`, `dy` | `1.15, 2.15` | Icon spacing (horizontal, vertical). |\n\n## Exercises\n\n1. Change `comp_kind` in `person_grid(n = 24, seed = 11)` to\n   `c(A = 0.1, B = 0.1, N = 0.8)` and re-render. What changes, and what\n   stays the same?\n2. Set `disabled_prop = 0` and re-render. Confirm it no longer errors\n   (per the `sample_counts()` discussion above) and check the `alt_text`\n   updates to match.\n3. Re-run the value-mode demo with `value = rnorm(24, mean = 2, sd = 1.2)`\n   (note the shifted mean) instead of `mean = 0`. Does `-2` still look as\n   \"strongly negative\" as it did before? Explain why, referring to how\n   `diverging_colors()` picks its scale.\n4. Try `ncol = 4` instead of `\"auto\"` on a grid of `n = 24` and compare\n   the result to the auto-chosen layout. When might you want to override\n   the automatic choice?\n5. Write a one-paragraph `fig-alt` description by hand for a `mode =\n   \"value\"` grid, then compare it to the `alt_text` the function\n   generates automatically. What would you add or change?\n\n## See also\n\n- [testing-sequence-walkthrough.qmd](testing-sequence-walkthrough.qmd) —\n  reuses `person_parts()`, `sample_counts()`, and `diverging_colors()` to\n  build a \"person at a computer\" icon.\n- [peer-review-workflow-walkthrough.qmd](peer-review-workflow-walkthrough.qmd) —\n  reuses the composite icon from `testing-sequence.R` (and so, transitively,\n  this script) for the author/editor/reviewer roles.\n"},"elapsedMs":14}</script></tool-result></div>

I read through [person-grid-walkthrough.qmd](person-grid-walkthrough.qmd) end to end. Overall it holds together well — the structure (aims → prerequisites → pipeline diagram → element-by-element → integration → exercises → see also) reads cleanly, and the code/prose balance is good (full function bodies shown only where the mechanics matter, prose summarizing the rest). A few specific things I'd flag before applying a pattern across the other four:

**Trim**
- The "`fig-alt` is auto-generated" point is made twice — once in step 6 of "Putting it together" (`alt_text` return value) and again under "Two things worth keeping in mind" in the integration section. Worth collapsing into one mention (probably keep it in the integration section, since that's where it's actionable, and just reference it briefly earlier).

**Expand**
- The `kind` parameter's three leg silhouettes (`"A"`/`"B"`/`"N"`, including the inclusive half-trouser/half-skirt design) is currently only described in prose. Since it's one of the more distinctive design decisions in the script, showing the actual branching code (a few lines) alongside the explanation would make it concrete rather than taking the summary on faith.
- No explicit "by the end of this you'll be able to..." framing under Aims — Heiss/Kurz-style walkthroughs often open with 2-3 concrete learning objectives. Right now Aims explains *what the script does*, but not quite *what the reader will be able to do*.
- The few error conditions a reader might actually trigger (the `stopifnot` in `sample_counts()`, the `value`-required-in-value-mode check) are mentioned individually in passing but never collected anywhere — a short "if you see this error" reference near the parameter table could be useful as a troubleshooting aid, especially for teachers working through the exercises unsupervised.

**Restyle**
- Several "worth knowing" asides (the diverging-colour symmetry caveat, the inclusive-silhouette rationale, the `disabled_prop = 0` fix) are currently plain paragraphs. These read like exactly the kind of thing a Quarto `.callout-note`/`.callout-tip` div is for — would make them visually scannable rather than blending into the surrounding prose.

Let me get your call on each before I touch the other four:

<div><tool-call id="toolu_01VNTWgXqN1Ttko6VBoKpwy3"><script type="application/json">{"version":2,"name":"edit","input":{"file_path":"person-grid-walkthrough.qmd","old_string":"Three design choices worth knowing about, because they explain shapes you\nmight otherwise ...[truncated, total 861 chars]","new_string":"Three design choices worth knowing about, because they explain shapes you\nmight otherwise ...[truncated, total 2035 chars]"},"caption":"person-grid-walkthrough.qmd","captionLink":"person-grid-walkthrough.qmd","inputPreview":{"type":"code","value":"@@ -108,12 +108,38 @@\n `testing-sequence.R`, next to a monitor icon) just by changing `ox`/`oy`.\n \n Three design choices worth knowing about, because they explain shapes you\n-might otherwise find surprising in the rendered figure:\n+might otherwise find surprising in the rendered figure. The most\n+distinctive is the leg silhouette, controlled by `kind`:\n \n-- `kind` controls the leg silhouette: `\"A\"` (trouser-coded, two separate leg\n-  bars), `\"B\"` (skirt-coded, one flared polygon), or `\"N\"` — literally half\n-  of each — an inclusive, non-binary-coded silhouette rather than a third\n-  stereotyped shape.\n+```{r}\n+#| label: show-kind-branching\n+#| eval: false\n+if (kind == \"A\") {           # trouser legs: two bars with a slim gap\n+  parts[[\"leg_l\"]] \u003c- data.frame(x = c(-0.20, -0.03, -0.06, -0.20),\n+                                  y = c(0.72, 0.72, 0.00, 0.00))\n+  parts[[\"leg_r\"]] \u003c- data.frame(x = c(0.03, 0.20, 0.20, 0.06),\n+                                  y = c(0.72, 0.72, 0.00, 0.00))\n+} else if (kind == \"B\") {    # skirt\n+  parts[[\"legs\"]] \u003c- data.frame(x = c(-0.20, 0.20, 0.46, -0.46),\n+                                 y = c(0.72, 0.72, 0.00, 0.00))\n+} else if (kind == \"N\") {    # half trouser (left) / half skirt (right)\n+  parts[[\"leg_l\"]] \u003c- data.frame(x = c(-0.20, -0.02, -0.05, -0.20),\n+                                  y = c(0.72, 0.72, 0.00, 0.00))\n+  parts[[\"legs_skirt_half\"]] \u003c- data.frame(x = c(0.00, 0.20, 0.46, 0.00),\n+                                            y = c(0.72, 0.72, 0.00, 0.00))\n+}\n+```\n+\n+::: {.callout-note title=\"Why a third silhouette, instead of just A/B?\"}\n+`\"A\"` is trouser-coded (two separate leg bars with a slim gap) and `\"B\"` is\n+skirt-coded (one flared polygon). Rather than picking one of those two as\n+a generic \"default\" person, `\"N\"` is literally half of each — an\n+inclusive, non-binary-coded silhouette, not a third stereotyped shape\n+layered on top of the first two.\n+:::\n+\n+The other two choices change less structurally:\n+\n - `disabled = TRUE` swaps the torso/legs for a single wheelchair wheel plus\n   a footrest peg, redesigned (per a code comment in the script) as one big\n   unbroken shape rather than several small ones, because small shapes stop\n","language":"diff"}}</script></tool-call></div>

<div><tool-call id="toolu_01EtntK2ugfc7cxPV7YqzBHc"><script type="application/json">{"version":2,"name":"edit","input":{"file_path":"person-grid-walkthrough.qmd","old_string":"This is the colour engine behind `person_grid()`'s `mode = \"value\"` —\ne.g. colouring icons...[truncated, total 652 chars]","new_string":"This is the colour engine behind `person_grid()`'s `mode = \"value\"` —\ne.g. colouring icons...[truncated, total 810 chars]"},"caption":"person-grid-walkthrough.qmd","captionLink":"person-grid-walkthrough.qmd","inputPreview":{"type":"code","value":"@@ -240,11 +240,16 @@\n (light, near-white), regardless of where your data happen to sit, and the\n two endpoints (default blue/orange — the Okabe–Ito colour-blind-safe\n palette, not a red/green or red/blue scale) are reached only at\n-`max(abs(value))`. That symmetry matters pedagogically: a `value` vector\n-of `c(-1, 3)` will *not* make `-1` look strongly negative, because `3` sets\n-the scale — worth knowing if your values aren't already roughly symmetric\n-around zero.\n+`max(abs(value))`.\n \n+::: {.callout-note title=\"The scale is set by whichever value is furthest from zero\"}\n+A `value` vector of `c(-1, 3)` will *not* make `-1` look strongly\n+negative, because `3` is furthest from zero and so sets the scale — `-1`\n+ends up only a third of the way to the \"strongly negative\" endpoint.\n+Worth checking if your values aren't already roughly symmetric around\n+zero.\n+:::\n+\n ```{r}\n #| label: demo-diverging-colors\n #| fig-cap: \"The diverging scale at a few sample values\"\n","language":"diff"}}</script></tool-call></div>

<div><tool-result call-id="toolu_019qgn1xpUr3ZV9LRnZns2oY"><script type="application/json">{"version":1,"status":"success","result":{"type":"text","value":"[File: 10.9KB, 305 lines, showing lines 1-305]\n1\t---\n2\ttitle: \"`testing-sequence.R`: one p...[truncated, total 12288 chars]"},"resultPreview":{"type":"code","value":"[File: 10.9KB, 305 lines, showing lines 1-305]\n---\ntitle: \"`testing-sequence.R`: one person, two people, a sample — at a computer\"\nsubtitle: \"Walkthrough: how the script works, and how to use it in Quarto\"\nformat:\n  html:\n    code-fold: false\n    toc: true\n---\n\n```{r}\n#| label: setup\n#| include: false\nknitr::opts_chunk$set(fig.align = \"center\")\n```\n\n## Aims\n\n`testing-sequence.R` extends the pictogram generator in `person-grid.R`\nwith a \"person sitting at a computer\" composite icon, and uses it to draw\nthe narrative sequence: **one** person doing a data-collection task → **two**\npeople doing it → a whole **sample** of people doing it (shown as a grid,\nwith no individual scores). It also provides a faceted \"grid of grids\"\nversion for showing several independent *studies*, each with its own\nsample of people. Paired with `sampling-distributions.R`'s synthetic score\nfigures, this is the icon half of a two-track illustration of sampling and\nmeasurement error.\n\n## Prerequisites / assumptions\n\n- **Packages**: `ggplot2` only.\n- **Sourcing**: `person-grid.R` **must** be sourced first — this script\n  reuses `person_parts()`, `sample_counts()`, and `diverging_colors()`\n  from it directly (not via a package namespace, so order matters).\n- **Reader background**: read\n  [person-grid-walkthrough.qmd](person-grid-walkthrough.qmd) first if the\n  underlying icon mechanics (geometry, composition sampling, colour modes)\n  aren't already familiar — this document assumes them.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: source-scripts\nsource(\"person-grid.R\")\nsource(\"testing-sequence.R\")\n```\n\n## How it works: the process\n\n```{mermaid}\nflowchart LR\n    A[\"person_parts()\\n(from person-grid.R)\"] --> C[\"person_at_computer()\\n(person + monitor, one role column)\"]\n    B[\"monitor_parts()\\n(screen geometry,\\npositioned relative to the head)\"] --> C\n    C --> D[\"testing_icons()\\nloop over n icons,\\nplace in a grid,\\noptional score labels\"]\n    D --> E[\"testing_icons_studies()\\nrepeat testing_icons() per study,\\nfacet_wrap(~study)\"]\n```\n\nThe key idea is **composition over inheritance**: rather than writing a new\nicon from scratch, `monitor_parts()` draws just the screen/stand, and\n`person_at_computer()` glues one `person_parts()` output and one\n`monitor_parts()` output into a single polygon set, tagged with a `role`\ncolumn (`\"person\"` vs `\"monitor\"`) so the two pieces can be filled\ndifferently. Everything above that (grids, score labels, faceted studies)\nis built the same way `person_grid()` was.\n\n## Element-by-element walkthrough\n\n### Screen geometry: `monitor_parts()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-monitor-parts\n#| eval: false\nmonitor_parts \u003c- function(scale = 1, ox = 0, oy = 0,\n                           center_y = 1.62, x_offset = 0.80) {\n  bezel_hw \u003c- 0.40\n  bezel_hh \u003c- 0.34\n  # ... bezel / screen / neck / base polygons, all offset from x_offset ...\n}\n```\n\nThis draws four stacked polygons (bezel, inset screen, neck, base — an\ninverted-T stand) in the **same local coordinate frame** `person_parts()`\nuses (head centred at `x = 0, y = 1.62`). Two defaults matter:\n\n- `center_y = 1.62` — the monitor is vertically centred *on the head*, not\n  on the whole body, so the composite reads as \"looking at a screen\"\n  rather than \"a person standing next to an unrelated box\".\n- `x_offset = 0.80` — a fixed horizontal gap from the body's centre line.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-monitor-parts\n#| fig-cap: \"monitor_parts() alone\"\nggplot(monitor_parts(), aes(x, y, group = part)) +\n  geom_polygon(fill = \"grey70\") +\n  coord_equal() +\n  theme_void()\n```\n\n### The composite icon: `person_at_computer()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-person-at-computer\n#| eval: false\nperson_at_computer \u003c- function(kind = \"A\", age = \"adult\", disabled = FALSE,\n                                scale = 1, ox = 0, oy = 0,\n                                person_color = \"#3B6EA5\",\n                                screen_color = \"#CFE8FF\",\n                                frame_color = \"#555555\") {\n  p \u003c- person_parts(kind, age, disabled, scale, ox, oy)\n  p$role \u003c- \"person\"\n  p$fill \u003c- person_color\n\n  m \u003c- monitor_parts(scale = scale, ox = ox, oy = oy)\n  m$role \u003c- \"monitor\"\n  m$fill \u003c- ifelse(m$part == \"screen\", screen_color, frame_color)\n\n  rbind(p, m)\n}\n```\n\nThis simply calls `person_parts()` and `monitor_parts()` with the *same*\n`scale`/`ox`/`oy`, stamps a `fill` colour on each (the monitor's bezel/neck/\nbase all get `frame_color`, only the inset `screen` part gets\n`screen_color`), and row-binds the two polygon sets. Because both pieces\nshare the same offset, the composite moves as one unit when placed in a\ngrid later.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-person-at-computer\n#| fig-cap: \"One composite icon\"\none_icon \u003c- person_at_computer(kind = \"A\")\nggplot(one_icon, aes(x, y, group = paste(part, role), fill = fill)) +\n  geom_polygon() +\n  scale_fill_identity() +\n  coord_equal() +\n  theme_void()\n```\n\n### The main sequence function: `testing_icons()`\n\nThis mirrors `person_grid()`'s structure closely (composition sampling,\ncolour modes, grid placement), with two differences specific to the\nnarrative sequence it's built for:\n\n- an optional `scores` argument: when supplied (length must equal `n`),\n  each icon gets an `\"x = \u003cscore>\"` label underneath it — used for the\n  one-person and two-person steps. Left `NULL` for the \"sample\" step,\n  where the narrative says scores exist but deliberately doesn't show\n  them.\n- it inherits `person_grid()`'s two colour modes (`\"diversity\"` /\n  `\"value\"`) unchanged, so the same colour-by-effect-size trick is\n  available here too, not just on plain person icons.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-testing-icons-one\n#| fig-cap: \"n = 1, with a score label\"\ntesting_icons(n = 1, scores = 27)$plot\n```\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-testing-icons-two\n#| fig-cap: \"n = 2, with score labels\"\ntesting_icons(n = 2, scores = c(31, 24))$plot\n```\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-testing-icons-sample\n#| fig-cap: \"n = 20, no scores shown -- 'a sample of people'\"\n#| fig-width: 7\n#| fig-height: 3.5\ntesting_icons(n = 20, seed = 7)$plot\n```\n\n### Many studies at once: `testing_icons_studies()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-testing-icons-studies\n#| eval: false\ntesting_icons_studies \u003c- function(n_studies, n_per_study, ncol = NULL,\n                                   seed = 1, ...) {\n  panels \u003c- vector(\"list\", n_studies)\n  for (s in seq_len(n_studies)) {\n    g \u003c- testing_icons(n = n_per_study, seed = seed + s, ...)\n    g$data$study \u003c- paste(\"Study\", s)\n    panels[[s]] \u003c- g$data\n  }\n  df \u003c- do.call(rbind, panels)\n  df$poly_id \u003c- paste(df$study, df$poly_id)\n  # ... ggplot() + facet_wrap(~study) ...\n}\n```\n\nThis is the icon analogue of `sampling-distributions.R`'s\n`plot_histogram_grid()`/`plot_dotpile_grid()`: it calls `testing_icons()`\nonce per \"study\" with an offset seed (`seed + s`), so each panel is an\n**independently drawn** sample (not the same 20 icons repeated), tags each\npanel's polygons with a `study` column, and facets. Note `poly_id` is\nre-prefixed with `study` — without this, icon 1 in Study 1 and icon 1 in\nStudy 2 would share a `poly_id` and `ggplot` would try to connect/group\nthem incorrectly across panels.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-testing-icons-studies\n#| fig-cap: \"Six independent 20-person samples ('studies')\"\n#| fig-width: 8\n#| fig-height: 5\ntesting_icons_studies(n_studies = 6, n_per_study = 20, ncol = 3, seed = 104)$plot\n```\n\n## Quarto usage / integration\n\nSetup-chunk lines (order matters):\n\n```r\nsource(\"person-grid.R\")\nsource(\"testing-sequence.R\")\n```\n\nReal usage from `doing-being-science.qmd` — one person, paired in a\ntwo-column layout with a `sampling-distributions.R` dot plot of the same\nsimulated score (`d_one$observed_score`), so the icon's label and the\npoint on the number line visibly match:\n\n````{verbatim}\n```{r}\n#| label: fig-one-person-icon\n#| fig-cap: \"You do the vocabulary test\"\n#| fig-width: 4\n#| fig-height: 3.75\n#| echo: false\n\nd_one \u003c- simulate_vocab_data(n = 1, seed = 101)\ntesting_icons(n = 1, scores = d_one$observed_score)$plot\n```\n````\n\nThe \"many studies\" step:\n\n````{verbatim}\n```{r}\n#| label: fig-many-studies-icons\n#| fig-width: 8\n#| fig-height: 5\n\ntesting_icons_studies(n_studies = 6, n_per_study = 20, ncol = 3, seed = 104)$plot\n```\n````\n\nA couple of integration notes:\n\n- The icon grid's `seed` only controls *cosmetic* sampling (silhouette,\n  age, disability, skin tone) — it has no bearing on simulated scores. If\n  you want an icon's label to match a score drawn elsewhere (as in the\n  one-person/two-person slides), pass that score in explicitly via\n  `scores =`, rather than relying on the seeds to line up.\n- `#| echo: false` is used on the slide chunks so only the figure shows on\n  the slide, not the simulation code — drop it if you want to show the\n  code for teaching purposes.\n\n### Parameter quick reference (`testing_icons()`)\n\n| Parameter | Default | Meaning |\n|---|---|---|\n| `n` | — | Number of icons. |\n| `scores` | `NULL` | Optional length-`n` vector; adds `\"x = \u003cscore>\"` labels. |\n| `ncol` | `NULL` (auto) | Icons per row. |\n| `comp_kind`, `comp_age`, `disabled_prop` | as `person_grid()` | Composition mix. |\n| `mode` | `\"diversity\"` | `\"diversity\"` or `\"value\"` (see `person-grid.R`). |\n| `skin_tones`, `value` | as `person_grid()` | Colour controls. |\n| `seed` | `1` | Cosmetic-sampling reproducibility (not score data). |\n| `dx`, `dy` | `1.9, 2.5` | Icon spacing. |\n\n`testing_icons_studies()` adds `n_studies`, `n_per_study`, and forwards\neverything else to `testing_icons()` via `...` (except `scores`, which\nisn't supported in the faceted version).\n\n## Exercises\n\n1. Call `testing_icons(n = 3, scores = c(20, 25, 30))` and check that the\n   label positions don't overlap. Then try `scores` of very different\n   lengths, e.g. `c(5, 38)` for `n = 2` — does label placement still\n   read clearly?\n2. In `monitor_parts()`, change `x_offset` from `0.80` to `0.40` and\n   re-render `person_at_computer()`. At what point does the monitor start\n   visually overlapping the body?\n3. Build a 12-study, 10-person-each grid with\n   `testing_icons_studies(n_studies = 12, n_per_study = 10, ncol = 4,\n   seed = 1)`. Compare it to `n_studies = 12, n_per_study = 10, ncol = 2`\n   — which layout reads better on a wide slide vs. a narrow one?\n4. `testing_icons()`'s `seed` only affects cosmetic sampling, not scores.\n   Demonstrate this: call it twice with the same `seed` but different\n   `scores`, and confirm the icons' composition (silhouette/age/skin\n   tone) is identical both times while only the labels differ.\n5. Try passing `mode = \"value\"` with a numeric `value` vector to\n   `testing_icons()` instead of the default diversity mode. What does an\n   icon-at-a-computer grid coloured by effect size communicate that a\n   plain `person_grid()` in value mode doesn't?\n\n## See also\n\n- [person-grid-walkthrough.qmd](person-grid-walkthrough.qmd) — the\n  dependency this script builds on; read first.\n- [sampling-distributions-walkthrough.qmd](sampling-distributions-walkthrough.qmd) —\n  the synthetic-data figures this icon sequence is paired with in the\n  slide deck (same narrative, data side instead of icon side).\n- [peer-review-workflow-walkthrough.qmd](peer-review-workflow-walkthrough.qmd) —\n  reuses `person_at_computer()` from this script.\n"},"elapsedMs":11}</script></tool-result></div>

<div><tool-result call-id="toolu_01UewLoQSoNv5V2RTXSia7xy"><script type="application/json">{"version":1,"status":"success","result":{"type":"text","value":"[File: 11.2KB, 289 lines, showing lines 1-289]\n1\t---\n2\ttitle: \"`peer-review-workflow.R`: t...[truncated, total 12522 chars]"},"resultPreview":{"type":"code","value":"[File: 11.2KB, 289 lines, showing lines 1-289]\n---\ntitle: \"`peer-review-workflow.R`: the peer-review cycle as a flow diagram\"\nsubtitle: \"Walkthrough: how the script works, and how to use it in Quarto\"\nformat:\n  html:\n    code-fold: false\n    toc: true\n---\n\n```{r}\n#| label: setup\n#| include: false\nknitr::opts_chunk$set(fig.align = \"center\")\n```\n\n## Aims\n\n`peer-review-workflow.R` draws a single, fixed diagram of the peer-reviewed\npublication cycle — author submits to editor, editor sends out to two\nreviewers (fan-out), reviewers report back (fan-in), editor decides, with a\ndashed loop-back arrow for \"reject with revise & resubmit → repeat\". It\nreuses the \"person at a computer\" icon from `testing-sequence.R` across\nfour roles (author, editor, reviewer ×2) rather than drawing a new icon\nstyle, so the peer-review figure visually matches the testing-sequence\nfigures elsewhere in the deck.\n\nUnlike the other four scripts in this set, this one is **not\nparametrized** for general reuse — it's a single bespoke diagram, built\nwith hand-placed coordinates for this specific six-icon layout. This\ndocument explains that layout logic so it can be adapted (e.g. a\ndifferent number of reviewers) rather than treated as a black box.\n\n## Prerequisites / assumptions\n\n- **Packages**: `ggplot2` only.\n- **Sourcing**: `person-grid.R` **and** `testing-sequence.R` must both be\n  sourced first, in that order — this script calls\n  `person_at_computer()` directly, which itself depends on\n  `person_parts()`/`monitor_parts()`.\n- **Reader background**: read\n  [person-grid-walkthrough.qmd](person-grid-walkthrough.qmd) and\n  [testing-sequence-walkthrough.qmd](testing-sequence-walkthrough.qmd)\n  first — this script is a thin, specific application of both.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: source-scripts\nsource(\"person-grid.R\")\nsource(\"testing-sequence.R\")\nsource(\"peer-review-workflow.R\")\n```\n\n## How it works: the process\n\n```{mermaid}\nflowchart TB\n    A[\"pos: named list of\\nhand-placed (x, y) coordinates\\nfor each of the 6 icons\"] --> B[\"icon_df() / icon helper\\nbuilds each icon via\\nperson_at_computer(), tags it,\\ncolours it by role\"]\n    A --> C[\"right_of() / left_of()\\ncompute arrow endpoints\\njust outside each icon,\\nnot at icon centres\"]\n    C --> D[\"straight_arrows, curve_arrows,\\nloop_arrow: data frames of\\narrow endpoints\"]\n    B --> E[\"single ggplot():\\nicons + segments + curves\\n+ role/step text labels\"]\n    D --> E\n```\n\nThe diagram is built from three largely independent ingredients that only\ncome together in the final `ggplot()` call: **where** each icon sits\n(`pos`), **what** each icon looks like (role-specific colour via\n`person_at_computer()`), and **how** the icons connect (arrows computed\nfrom positions, not drawn freehand).\n\n## Element-by-element walkthrough\n\n### Layout: the `pos` list and role colours\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-pos\n#| eval: false\nresearcher_col \u003c- list(person = \"#3B6EA5\", screen = \"#CFE8FF\")\neditor_col     \u003c- list(person = \"#B0562D\", screen = \"#FFDCC5\")\nreviewer_col   \u003c- list(person = \"#3B9E5F\", screen = \"#D3F5DF\")\n\npos \u003c- list(\n  researcher1 = c(0,    0),\n  editor1     = c(5.0,  0),\n  reviewerA   = c(10.5, 3.4),\n  reviewerB   = c(10.5, -3.4),\n  editor2     = c(16.0, 0),\n  researcher2 = c(21.0, 0)\n)\n```\n\nEvery coordinate here is hand-chosen, not computed from a formula (unlike\n`person-grid.R`'s automatic grid spacing). The author and editor appear\n**twice** (`researcher1`/`researcher2`, `editor1`/`editor2`) at different\nx-positions, because the same two people appear at both the start and end\nof the cycle — this is a flow diagram of a *process*, not a single\nsnapshot of who's present. The two reviewers are offset vertically\n(`+3.4`/`-3.4`) at the same x-position, to draw the fan-out/fan-in\nvisually as a split-and-rejoin. Each role gets its own `person`/`screen`\ncolour pair, reused across every icon that plays that role.\n\n### Building each icon: the `icon_df()` helper\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-icon-df\n#| eval: false\nicon_df \u003c- function(name, kind, cols) {\n  p \u003c- pos[[name]]\n  d \u003c- person_at_computer(kind = kind, ox = p[1], oy = p[2],\n                           person_color = cols$person, screen_color = cols$screen)\n  d$icon \u003c- name\n  d\n}\n\nicons \u003c- rbind(\n  icon_df(\"researcher1\", \"A\", researcher_col),\n  icon_df(\"editor1\",     \"B\", editor_col),\n  icon_df(\"reviewerA\",   \"A\", reviewer_col),\n  icon_df(\"reviewerB\",   \"B\", reviewer_col),\n  icon_df(\"editor2\",     \"B\", editor_col),\n  icon_df(\"researcher2\", \"A\", researcher_col)\n)\nicons$poly_id \u003c- paste(icons$icon, icons$part, icons$role)\n```\n\nThis is a direct application of `person_at_computer()` (from\n`testing-sequence.R`) at each named position, looked up from `pos`. The\n`kind` argument (`\"A\"`/`\"B\"`) here is just varying the leg silhouette for\nvisual variety across the six icons — it carries no role meaning (unlike\n`person_color`/`screen_color`, which *does* encode role). As with every\nother script in this set, `poly_id` is rebuilt to be unique per icon\n*and* per sub-part, so `ggplot`'s `group` aesthetic doesn't connect\npolygons that happen to share a part name (e.g. every icon's `\"head\"`)\nacross different icons.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-icon-df\n#| fig-cap: \"The six positioned icons, before any arrows are added\"\nresearcher_col \u003c- list(person = \"#3B6EA5\", screen = \"#CFE8FF\")\neditor_col     \u003c- list(person = \"#B0562D\", screen = \"#FFDCC5\")\nreviewer_col   \u003c- list(person = \"#3B9E5F\", screen = \"#D3F5DF\")\npos \u003c- list(\n  researcher1 = c(0, 0), editor1 = c(5.0, 0),\n  reviewerA = c(10.5, 3.4), reviewerB = c(10.5, -3.4),\n  editor2 = c(16.0, 0), researcher2 = c(21.0, 0)\n)\nicon_df \u003c- function(name, kind, cols) {\n  p \u003c- pos[[name]]\n  d \u003c- person_at_computer(kind = kind, ox = p[1], oy = p[2],\n                           person_color = cols$person, screen_color = cols$screen)\n  d$icon \u003c- name\n  d\n}\ndemo_icons \u003c- rbind(\n  icon_df(\"researcher1\", \"A\", researcher_col),\n  icon_df(\"editor1\", \"B\", editor_col),\n  icon_df(\"reviewerA\", \"A\", reviewer_col),\n  icon_df(\"reviewerB\", \"B\", reviewer_col),\n  icon_df(\"editor2\", \"B\", editor_col),\n  icon_df(\"researcher2\", \"A\", researcher_col)\n)\ndemo_icons$poly_id \u003c- paste(demo_icons$icon, demo_icons$part, demo_icons$role)\nggplot(demo_icons, aes(x, y, group = poly_id, fill = fill)) +\n  geom_polygon() +\n  scale_fill_identity() +\n  coord_equal() +\n  theme_void()\n```\n\n### Arrows: `right_of()`/`left_of()` and three arrow data frames\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-arrow-helpers\n#| eval: false\nright_of \u003c- function(name, dy = 0.9) c(pos[[name]][1] + 1.25, pos[[name]][2] + dy)\nleft_of  \u003c- function(name, dy = 0.9) c(pos[[name]][1] - 0.55, pos[[name]][2] + dy)\n```\n\nArrows are anchored just outside each icon (`+1.25`/`-0.55` from the\nicon's x-position, with a small `+0.9` vertical nudge) rather than at icon\ncentres — centring the arrows would draw them straight through the icon\npolygons. Three kinds of connections are built from these anchors:\n\n- `straight_arrows` — plain `geom_segment()` arrows: author → editor, and\n  editor → author (submission and final decision).\n- `curve_arrows` — `geom_curve()` arrows with an explicit `curvature`\n  value per row (`+0.30` or `-0.30`) for the fan-out (editor → two\n  reviewers) and fan-in (two reviewers → editor) — the sign of `curvature`\n  controls which way each curve bows, matching the reviewer's vertical\n  offset so the curves don't cross awkwardly.\n- `loop_arrow` — a single dashed `geom_curve()` well below the main row\n  (`y = -7.0`), representing \"reject, revise & resubmit\" looping back from\n  the end to the start.\n\n### Text labels and final assembly\n\nTwo more data frames (`step_captions`, `role_labels`) place bold numbered\nstep captions (e.g. \"1. Submit manuscript\") and small role labels\n(\"Author\", \"Editor\", \"Reviewer 1\"/\"2\") at further hand-offset positions. A\ncode comment in the script flags that *Reviewer 1*'s label needed a\ndifferent vertical offset than the generic pattern to clear its icon\nwithout crowding the step caption beneath it — a reminder that this\ndiagram's positions were tuned by eye, so changing icon spacing or icon\nsize will likely require re-tuning label offsets too, rather than\ncascading automatically.\n\nEverything (polygons, segments, curves, loop arrow, annotation, two sets\nof text labels) is layered onto **one** `ggplot()` object and returned as\n`peer_review_workflow()$plot`.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-peer-review-workflow\n#| fig-cap: \"The full peer-review workflow diagram\"\n#| fig-width: 9\n#| fig-height: 6\npeer_review_workflow()$plot\n```\n\n## Quarto usage / integration\n\nSetup-chunk lines (order matters: both dependencies before this script):\n\n```r\nsource(\"person-grid.R\")\nsource(\"testing-sequence.R\")\nsource(\"peer-review-workflow.R\")\n```\n\nReal usage from `doing-being-science.qmd`:\n\n````{verbatim}\n```{r}\n#| label: fig-peer-review-workflow\n#| fig-cap: \"The peer-reviewed publication cycle: submission, review, and decision, repeating until accept or reject\"\n#| fig-width: 9\n#| fig-height: 6\n\npeer_review_workflow()$plot\n```\n````\n\n`peer_review_workflow()` takes **no arguments** — there's nothing to\nconfigure via chunk options beyond `fig-width`/`fig-height` (the plot uses\n`coord_equal(clip = \"off\", ylim = c(-8.8, 5.8))` internally, so it has a\nfixed aspect ratio; picking a `fig-width`/`fig-height` far from roughly\n9:6 will add letterboxing rather than reflowing the diagram).\n\n### If you need to adapt this diagram\n\nBecause nothing here is parametrized, adapting it (e.g. three reviewers\ninstead of two, or a different number of decision rounds) means editing\nthe script directly rather than passing new arguments:\n\n1. Add/remove entries in `pos` (and give any new role a colour list like\n   `reviewer_col`).\n2. Add/remove the corresponding `icon_df()` calls.\n3. Add/remove rows in `curve_arrows` for the new fan-out/fan-in\n   connections, with signed `curvature` chosen to avoid crossing.\n4. Re-check `role_labels`/`step_captions` y-offsets by eye — as noted\n   above, these were tuned per-icon, not computed generically.\n\n## Exercises\n\n1. Re-render `peer_review_workflow()$plot` at `fig-width = 6, fig-height\n   = 6` (narrower than the 9:6 the diagram was designed for). What\n   happens, and why (hint: look at the internal `coord_equal(clip =\n   \"off\", ylim = ...)` call)?\n2. Add a third reviewer: duplicate `reviewerB`'s entry in `pos` as\n   `reviewerC` at a third vertical offset, add a matching `icon_df()`\n   call, and add the corresponding fan-out/fan-in rows to\n   `curve_arrows`. What else needs adjusting before it looks right?\n3. Change `reviewer_col`'s `person` colour and re-render. Which parts of\n   the diagram change colour, and which don't? Explain by tracing how\n   `person_at_computer()` uses `person_color` vs. `screen_color`.\n4. The script notes Reviewer 1's label offset needed manual tuning.\n   Deliberately break it (set its `y` back to the generic `+1.4` the\n   code comment mentions) and re-render — confirm you can see why the\n   fix was needed.\n5. This script takes no arguments at all. Sketch (in words, or in code if\n   you want to go further) what a parametrized version might look like —\n   e.g. `peer_review_workflow(n_reviewers = 3)` — and what would have to\n   become programmatic (positions? arrows? labels?) rather than\n   hand-placed.\n\n## See also\n\n- [testing-sequence-walkthrough.qmd](testing-sequence-walkthrough.qmd) —\n  the source of the `person_at_computer()` icon reused four times here.\n- [person-grid-walkthrough.qmd](person-grid-walkthrough.qmd) — the\n  underlying geometry/colour primitives.\n"},"elapsedMs":8}</script></tool-result></div>

<div><tool-result call-id="toolu_01PrRRufCUJCjqc8h37GpwVn"><script type="application/json">{"version":1,"status":"success","result":{"type":"text","value":"[File: 14.9KB, 401 lines, showing lines 1-401]\n1\t---\n2\ttitle: \"`sampling-distributions.R`:...[truncated, total 16701 chars]"},"resultPreview":{"type":"code","value":"[File: 14.9KB, 401 lines, showing lines 1-401]\n---\ntitle: \"`sampling-distributions.R`: simulating and plotting test scores\"\nsubtitle: \"Walkthrough: how the script works, and how to use it in Quarto\"\nformat:\n  html:\n    code-fold: false\n    toc: true\n---\n\n```{r}\n#| label: setup\n#| include: false\nknitr::opts_chunk$set(fig.align = \"center\")\n```\n\n## Aims\n\n`sampling-distributions.R` simulates scores on a hypothetical 0–40 item\nvocabulary test, from the trial (item) level up, and draws the synthetic\ndata half of the sampling/measurement-error narrative: one dot → two dots\n→ a \"pile of dots\" showing a sample's distribution → a histogram → a grid\nof histograms (repeated studies) → a scatterplot against a continuous\ncovariate. It's the data-generating and plotting counterpart to\n`testing-sequence.R`'s icon sequence (same narrative, numbers instead of\npictograms).\n\nThe simulation is deliberately built **trial-level up** rather than\ngenerating a continuous score and rounding/clipping it: every simulated\nperson's score is the sum of 40 independent correct/incorrect item\nresponses, so it is *structurally* impossible for a simulated score to\nfall outside `[0, 40]` — there's no clipping step anywhere in the script,\nbecause none is needed.\n\n## Prerequisites / assumptions\n\n- **Packages**: `ggplot2`, and `ggdist` for the dot-pile figures\n  (`plot_dotpile()`, `plot_dotpile_grid()`).\n- **Sourcing**: standalone — no other script in this set needs to be\n  sourced first.\n- **Reader background**: some familiarity with classical test theory\n  terms (reliability, true score) is helpful but not required — the key\n  ideas are explained below. Basic `ggplot2` familiarity assumed.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: source-script\nlibrary(ggdist)\nsource(\"sampling-distributions.R\")\n```\n\n## How it works: the process\n\n```{r}\n#| label: model-equation\n#| echo: false\n#| results: asis\ncat(\n  \"$$\",\n  \"\\\\text{Ability (logit scale):} \\\\quad \\\\theta_i \\\\sim N(\\\\theta_\\\\mu, \\\\theta_\\\\sigma)\",\n  \"$$\",\n  \"$$\",\n  \"\\\\text{Per-item probability:} \\\\quad p_i = \\\\text{plogis}(\\\\theta_i)\",\n  \"$$\",\n  \"$$\",\n  \"\\\\text{Observed score:} \\\\quad Y_i \\\\sim \\\\text{Binomial}(40, p_i) \\\\quad \\\\in \\\\{0, \\\\dots, 40\\\\}\",\n  \"$$\",\n  sep = \"\\n\"\n)\n```\n\nA `Binomial(40, p_i)` draw is the vectorized equivalent of summing 40\nindependent Bernoulli (correct/incorrect) trials, so the model above\n*is* the trial-level process described in the aims, just computed\nefficiently rather than looped item-by-item.\n\n```{mermaid}\nflowchart LR\n    A[\"calibrate_ability()\\nsolve theta_mu, theta_sigma\\nto hit target mu + reliability\\n(or mu + sd_true)\"] --> B[\"simulate_vocab_data()\\ndraw theta_i per person,\\np_i = plogis(theta_i),\\nscore_i ~ Binomial(40, p_i)\"]\n    B --> C[\"plot_dots() / plot_dotpile() /\\nplot_histogram() / plot_scatter()\"]\n    B --> D[\"simulate_studies()\\nrepeat simulate_vocab_data()\\nper independent 'study'\"]\n    D --> E[\"plot_histogram_grid() /\\nplot_dotpile_grid()\\n(facet_wrap(~study))\"]\n```\n\nThe central design idea: **you don't set the ability distribution's mean\nand spread directly.** You set the things that matter pedagogically — the\ntest's mean score (`mu`) and its reliability (`reliability`, or\nalternatively a target spread `sd_true`) — and `calibrate_ability()`\nnumerically solves for the underlying `theta_mu`/`theta_sigma` that\nreproduce them, because for a fixed test length, mean/reliability/spread\nare mathematically linked, not three independently free knobs.\n\n## Element-by-element walkthrough\n\n### Calibration machinery: `expect_theta()` and `implied_moments()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-expect-theta\n#| eval: false\nexpect_theta \u003c- function(f, theta_mu, theta_sigma) {\n  integrate(function(theta) f(theta) * dnorm(theta, theta_mu, theta_sigma),\n            lower = theta_mu - 10 * theta_sigma,\n            upper = theta_mu + 10 * theta_sigma)$value\n}\n```\n\n`expect_theta()` computes $E[f(\\theta)]$ for $\\theta \\sim N(\\theta_\\mu,\n\\theta_\\sigma)$ by plain numerical integration (base R `integrate()`, no\nextra dependency) — used to get exact expectations like $E[p_i]$ or\n$E[p_i^2]$ without resorting to Monte Carlo.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-implied-moments\n#| eval: false\nimplied_moments \u003c- function(theta_mu, theta_sigma, n_items) {\n  p_bar  \u003c- expect_theta(plogis, theta_mu, theta_sigma)\n  p2_bar \u003c- expect_theta(function(th) plogis(th)^2, theta_mu, theta_sigma)\n  pq_bar \u003c- expect_theta(function(th) plogis(th) * (1 - plogis(th)), theta_mu, theta_sigma)\n\n  mean_score  \u003c- n_items * p_bar\n  var_true    \u003c- n_items^2 * (p2_bar - p_bar^2)\n  var_error   \u003c- n_items * pq_bar\n  reliability \u003c- var_true / (var_true + var_error)\n  # ...\n}\n```\n\nGiven a candidate `(theta_mu, theta_sigma)`, this works out what mean\nscore, \"true\"-score variance (between-person spread), measurement-error\nvariance (within-person, item-sampling noise), and reliability\n(`var_true / (var_true + var_error)`) that choice *implies* — i.e. it runs\nthe model forward analytically. `calibrate_ability()` then runs this\nbackward: searching for the `(theta_mu, theta_sigma)` whose\n`implied_moments()` output matches your targets.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-implied-moments\nimplied_moments(theta_mu = 0, theta_sigma = 1.3, n_items = 40)\n```\n\n### Solving for ability parameters: `calibrate_ability()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-calibrate-ability-signature\n#| eval: false\ncalibrate_ability \u003c- function(mu, reliability = 0.80, sd_true = NULL, n_items = 40) {\n  # ...\n}\n```\n\nTwo calibration targets are supported, and they're **mutually\nexclusive** — for a fixed `mu` and `n_items`, fixing one determines the\nother:\n\n- `reliability` (the default path) — \"how repeatable is this test\"; use\n  this when the figure is about test-retest/parallel-forms reliability.\n- `sd_true` — \"how spread out is this population\"; use this when a figure\n  needs independent control of between-person spread (e.g. two panels\n  with the same mean but visibly different spread), supplied instead of\n  `reliability`.\n\nInternally, both paths use the same robust pattern: a nested `uniroot()`\nsearch (an outer search over `theta_sigma`, and for each candidate, an\ninner search over `theta_mu` so the mean score matches `mu` exactly), with\nthe search bracket doubled automatically if the initial range doesn't\ncontain a root, and an informative error (rather than a cryptic numerical\nfailure) if the target isn't achievable — e.g. `reliability` above about\n0.99 would require an implausibly large, numerically unstable ability\nspread for a 40-item test. `sd_true` additionally has a closed-form\nfeasibility check: the theoretical ceiling is `sqrt(mu * (n_items - mu))`,\napproached only as the ability spread goes to infinity.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-calibrate-ability\ncal \u003c- calibrate_ability(mu = 30, reliability = 0.80)\ncal$moments\n```\n\n### Simulating one sample: `simulate_vocab_data()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-simulate-vocab-data-core\n#| eval: false\ncal \u003c- calibrate_ability(mu, reliability = reliability, sd_true = sd_true, n_items = n_items)\n# ... correlation setup (see below) ...\ntheta \u003c- cal$theta_mu + cal$theta_sigma * theta_z\np_correct \u003c- plogis(theta)\nobserved_score \u003c- rbinom(n, size = n_items, prob = p_correct)\n```\n\nAfter calibration, simulating is simple: draw standardized ability\n`theta_z` per person (optionally correlated with a covariate — see next\nsection), scale/shift it to `theta`, convert to `p_correct` via\n`plogis()`, and draw `observed_score` as a single vectorized\n`rbinom(n, 40, p_correct)` call — bounded to `[0, 40]` by construction.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-simulate-vocab-data\nd \u003c- simulate_vocab_data(n = 10, mu = 30, reliability = 0.80, seed = 1)\nd\nrange(simulate_vocab_data(n = 10000, seed = 2)$observed_score)\n```\n\n### Inducing a covariate correlation: `r_with_x`\n\nThis is the part of the script most worth reading carefully if you're\nadapting it, because of **attenuation**: any correlation involving a\nnoisy *observed* variable is weaker than the correlation involving the\nunderlying *true* ability, because measurement error adds noise that\ndilutes the relationship. Classical test theory gives\n$\\text{corr}(\\text{observed}, \\theta) = \\sqrt{\\text{reliability}}$, so to\nhit a target **observed**-score correlation `r_with_x`, the script works\nbackward through that relationship:\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-attenuation-logic\n#| eval: false\nmax_r \u003c- sqrt(reliability)\nif (abs(r_with_x) > max_r) {\n  warning(...)  # cap r_with_x at the achievable ceiling\n  r_with_x \u003c- sign(r_with_x) * max_r\n}\nr_theta_x \u003c- if (r_with_x == 0) 0 else r_with_x / max_r\n# theta_z is then a mix of standardized x and independent noise,\n# correlated with x at r_theta_x so that, after measurement error,\n# the *observed* score ends up correlated with x at (approximately) r_with_x\n```\n\nTwo consequences worth knowing: (1) `r_with_x` is specified on the\n*observed*-score scale — i.e. it's (approximately) the correlation you'll\nactually see in `plot_scatter()`, not some larger hidden value; (2) at\nlow `reliability`, only a limited range of `r_with_x` is achievable at\nall (capped at `sqrt(reliability)`), and the function warns rather than\nsilently failing if you ask for more than that.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-r-with-x\nd_cor \u003c- simulate_vocab_data(n = 2000, r_with_x = 0.5, seed = 3)\ncor(d_cor$x, d_cor$observed_score)\n```\n\nOne more detail handled explicitly: `scale(x)` is undefined (`NaN`) when\n`n \u003c 2` or `x` has zero variance, and `0 * NaN` is `NaN` in R — so the\nstandardization path is skipped entirely whenever `r_theta_x == 0`,\npreventing an `NA` score from leaking in even at the default\n`r_with_x = 0` with `n = 1`.\n\n### Repeated studies: `simulate_studies()` and `simulate_parallel_forms()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-simulate-studies\nstudies \u003c- simulate_studies(n_studies = 3, n_per_study = 15, seed = 4)\ntapply(studies$observed_score, studies$study, mean)\n```\n\n`simulate_studies()` just calls `simulate_vocab_data()` once per study\n(without resetting the seed between calls, so each study's sample differs\nbut the whole sequence stays reproducible from one top-level `seed`),\nrow-binding the results with a `study` label — this is what feeds the\n`_grid()` plotting functions below.\n\n`simulate_parallel_forms()` is a different use case: it draws **one**\n`theta_i` per person but **two** independent test administrations\n(`form1`, `form2`) from that same `theta_i` — i.e. the same person tested\ntwice (or on two equivalent forms). This is how the script's own\nreliability calibration can be checked empirically:\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-parallel-forms\npf \u003c- simulate_parallel_forms(n = 20000, mu = 30, reliability = 0.80, seed = 5)\ncor(pf$form1, pf$form2)\n```\n\n### Figures: `plot_dots()`, `plot_dotpile()`, `plot_histogram()`, `plot_scatter()`, and their `_grid()` variants\n\nThese are comparatively simple `ggplot2` wrappers; the interesting parts\nare the choices, not the mechanics:\n\n- `plot_dots()` — one or two scores as points on a number line, with `y`\n  nudged just above 0 so points sit on the axis line rather than being\n  bisected by it.\n- `plot_dotpile()` — a sample's distribution as discrete points via\n  `ggdist::stat_dots()`, by design **not** a smoothed density curve or a\n  binned histogram, so the figure reads as \"these are the actual\n  individual scores\", not an idealized shape.\n- `plot_dotpile_grid()` — the faceted version; `binwidth` is fixed\n  explicitly rather than left to `ggdist`'s per-panel default, because\n  that default sizes each panel's dot diameter to that panel's own\n  tallest stack — which would make dots visibly different sizes across\n  panels, undermining a \"same scale, different samples\" comparison.\n- `plot_histogram()` / `plot_histogram_grid()` — standard binned\n  histograms, for the \"distribution looks approximately normal\" step.\n- `plot_scatter()` — score against a covariate, with an optional\n  `geom_smooth(method = \"lm\")` fitted line.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-figures\n#| fig-width: 7\n#| fig-height: 2.2\nplot_dots(d$observed_score[1])\n```\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-dotpile\n#| fig-width: 7\n#| fig-height: 2.5\nplot_dotpile(simulate_vocab_data(n = 30, seed = 6)$observed_score)\n```\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-histogram-grid\n#| fig-width: 8\n#| fig-height: 4\nplot_histogram_grid(simulate_studies(6, 40, seed = 7))\n```\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-scatter\n#| fig-width: 6\n#| fig-height: 4\nplot_scatter(simulate_vocab_data(n = 80, r_with_x = 0.5, seed = 8))\n```\n\n## Quarto usage / integration\n\nSetup-chunk lines:\n\n```r\nlibrary(ggdist)\nsource(\"sampling-distributions.R\")\n```\n\nReal usage from `doing-being-science.qmd` — paired with\n`testing-sequence.R`'s icon, sharing one `simulate_vocab_data()` draw so\nthe icon's score label and the plotted point match:\n\n````{verbatim}\n```{r}\n#| label: fig-one-score-dot\n#| fig-cap: \"Their score, as a point on the number line\"\n#| fig-width: 5\n#| fig-height: 3.2\n#| echo: false\n\nplot_dots(d_one$observed_score)\n```\n````\n\nThe repeated-studies pairing (icon grid + matching dotpile grid):\n\n````{verbatim}\n```{r}\n#| label: fig-many-studies-dotpiles\n#| fig-width: 8\n#| fig-height: 5\n\nplot_dotpile_grid(studies_sample, ncol = 3)\n```\n````\n\n### Parameter quick reference (`simulate_vocab_data()`)\n\n| Parameter | Default | Meaning |\n|---|---|---|\n| `n` | — | Sample size. |\n| `mu` | `30` | Target mean observed score (0–40 scale). |\n| `reliability` | `0.80` | Target parallel-forms reliability; ignored if `sd_true` given. |\n| `sd_true` | `NULL` | Alternate calibration target: between-person \"true\" score SD. |\n| `r_with_x` | `0` | Target correlation between the covariate `x` and the **observed** score. |\n| `x`, `x_mean`, `x_sd` | `NULL, 0, 1` | Pre-specified covariate, or generated from `N(x_mean, x_sd)`. |\n| `n_items` | `40` | Number of test items. |\n| `seed` | `NULL` | Reproducibility. |\n\n## Exercises\n\n1. Call `calibrate_ability(mu = 30, reliability = 0.95)` and then\n   `calibrate_ability(mu = 30, reliability = 0.99)`. Compare the two\n   `$moments$sd_true` values. What happens if you push `reliability`\n   toward `0.999`, and why does the script's error message call that\n   \"implausibly large\"?\n2. Simulate two samples with the same `mu` and `n` but different\n   `reliability` (e.g. `0.6` vs `0.95`), and plot both with\n   `plot_dotpile()`. Which one's spread looks bigger, and does that\n   match what `implied_moments()` predicts?\n3. Request `r_with_x = 0.9` at `reliability = 0.5` via\n   `simulate_vocab_data()`. Confirm it warns and is capped, and compute\n   `sqrt(0.5)` by hand to check the cap matches.\n4. Use `sd_true` instead of `reliability` to calibrate two samples with\n   the *same* spread but different means (e.g. `mu = 20` vs `mu = 35`,\n   both `sd_true = 5`). Plot both as histograms side by side — do they\n   look like plausible vocabulary-test distributions at both means?\n5. Generate 6 studies with `simulate_studies(6, 15, seed = 1)` (a small\n   `n` per study) and compare `plot_histogram_grid()` against\n   `plot_dotpile_grid()` for the same data. Which representation makes\n   the sample-to-sample variability at small `n` easier to see, and why?\n\n## See also\n\n- [testing-sequence-walkthrough.qmd](testing-sequence-walkthrough.qmd) —\n  the icon-based half of the same sampling/measurement-error narrative;\n  several slides pair one figure from each script using a shared\n  simulated dataset.\n"},"elapsedMs":9}</script></tool-result></div>

<div><tool-result call-id="toolu_014uXTCEAQBvXCbUdVt6jo6v"><script type="application/json">{"version":1,"status":"success","result":{"type":"text","value":"[File: 12.5KB, 329 lines, showing lines 1-329]\n1\t---\n2\ttitle: \"`concept-map.R`: many-to-ma...[truncated, total 13987 chars]"},"resultPreview":{"type":"code","value":"[File: 12.5KB, 329 lines, showing lines 1-329]\n---\ntitle: \"`concept-map.R`: many-to-many column diagrams\"\nsubtitle: \"Walkthrough: how the script works, and how to use it in Quarto\"\nformat:\n  html:\n    code-fold: false\n    toc: true\n---\n\n```{r}\n#| label: setup\n#| include: false\nknitr::opts_chunk$set(fig.align = \"center\")\n```\n\n## Aims\n\n`concept-map.R` draws a general \"N columns of ellipses, many-to-many\ncurved links between adjacent columns\" diagram, in the style of\nMcElreath's *Statistical Rethinking* Figure 1.2 (theories → process\nmodels → statistical models). It's used in `doing-being-science.qmd` to\nmake the point that the theory is not the hypothesis is not the\nstatistical test result: several different theories can imply the same\nprocess model, and several different process models can imply the same\nstatistical test result, so a single statistically significant result\nnever uniquely confirms one theory.\n\nUnlike `person-grid.R`/`testing-sequence.R`, this script isn't about\npictograms at all — it's a general-purpose node-and-edge diagram builder\nthat happens to use ellipses and curves rather than a graph library, so it\nrenders identically wherever Quarto builds the document.\n\n## Prerequisites / assumptions\n\n- **Packages**: `ggplot2` only.\n- **Sourcing**: standalone — no other script in this set needs to be\n  sourced first.\n- **Reader background**: no special background needed beyond basic\n  `ggplot2` (`geom_polygon()`, `geom_curve()`).\n\n```{r}\n#| label: source-script\nsource(\"concept-map.R\")\n```\n\n## How it works: the process\n\n```{mermaid}\nflowchart LR\n    A[\"columns: list of\\ncharacter vectors\\n(node labels per column)\"] --> D[\"validate edges\\n(catch typo'd indices early)\"]\n    B[\"edges: list of\\nfrom/to index data frames,\\none per column gap\"] --> D\n    C[\"rx, ry, wrap_width, dx, dy:\\nsizing + spacing\\n(global or per-column)\"] --> E[\"lay out nodes:\\none column at a time,\\ncentred vertically\"]\n    D --> F[\"compute edge endpoints\\nat each node's own\\nellipse boundary\"]\n    E --> F\n    F --> G[\"ggplot():\\ngeom_curve() edges +\\ngeom_polygon() ellipses +\\ngeom_text() labels\"]\n```\n\nThe structure mirrors a typical graph-drawing pipeline: nodes are laid out\nfirst (purely from `columns` + sizing parameters, independent of edges),\nthen edges are computed as line segments between specific node\nboundaries, then both are drawn in one plot. Keeping layout and edges\nseparate is what makes \"many-to-many\" straightforward — an edge is just\n`(from index in column k, to index in column k+1)`, resolved to actual\ncoordinates only at the end.\n\n## Element-by-element walkthrough\n\n### Geometry helpers: `ellipse_pts()` and `wrap_label()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-ellipse-pts\n#| eval: false\nellipse_pts \u003c- function(cx, cy, rx, ry, n = 72) {\n  t \u003c- seq(0, 2 * pi, length.out = n)\n  data.frame(x = cx + rx * cos(t), y = cy + ry * sin(t))\n}\n\nwrap_label \u003c- function(label, width = 16) {\n  paste(strwrap(label, width = width), collapse = \"\\n\")\n}\n```\n\n`ellipse_pts()` is the ellipse analogue of `person-grid.R`'s\n`circle_pts()` — a centre, two radii (`rx`, `ry`), and a point count.\n`wrap_label()` wraps long node text (e.g. full theory sentences) onto\nmultiple lines at a given character width, using base R's `strwrap()`,\nsince node labels here are often full sentences rather than short tags.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-wrap-label\ncat(wrap_label(\"Words we experience more often are easier to recall\", width = 16))\n```\n\n### Per-column parameters: `recycle_per_column()`\n\nA design choice specific to this script: `rx`, `ry`, and `wrap_width` can\neach be given as **either** a single value (applied to every column) or a\nvector with one entry per column:\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-recycle\n#| eval: false\nrecycle_per_column \u003c- function(val, name) {\n  if (length(val) == 1) return(rep(val, k))\n  if (length(val) == k) return(val)\n  stop(sprintf(\"`%s` must have length 1 or length(columns) = %d, not %d.\",\n               name, k, length(val)))\n}\n```\n\nThis matters in practice because columns rarely hold equally long labels —\ne.g. a \"theories\" column of full sentences next to a \"statistical tests\"\ncolumn of short phrases needs a bigger ellipse (and wider wrap width) on\nthe first column, not a one-size-fits-all setting.\n\n### Validation before layout: edge-index checking\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-edge-validation\n#| eval: false\nfor (ci in seq_len(k - 1)) {\n  e \u003c- edges[[ci]]\n  n_from \u003c- length(columns[[ci]])\n  n_to \u003c- length(columns[[ci + 1]])\n  bad_from \u003c- e$from[!(e$from %in% seq_len(n_from))]\n  bad_to \u003c- e$to[!(e$to %in% seq_len(n_to))]\n  if (length(bad_from) > 0) {\n    stop(sprintf(\n      \"edges[[%d]]$from has out-of-range value(s) %s: column %d ('%s') only has %d item(s), so valid indices are 1..%d.\",\n      ci, paste(unique(bad_from), collapse = \", \"), ci,\n      if (!is.null(titles)) titles[ci] else paste(\"column\", ci), n_from, n_from\n    ))\n  }\n  # ... same check for `to` ...\n}\n```\n\nEvery `from`/`to` index in `edges` is checked against the actual length of\nthe relevant column *before* any plotting is attempted. This is worth\ncalling out because the failure mode it avoids is subtle: an out-of-range\nindex used directly as a lookup would otherwise silently produce `NA`\ncoordinates, and the edge would simply vanish from the rendered plot with\nonly a generic, hard-to-trace `ggplot2` warning — rather than an error\nthat names the exact column and bad index.\n\nA similar check (not shown in full here) warns if `dx` is too small for\ntwo neighbouring columns' `rx` values, which would otherwise manifest as\nvisibly overlapping ellipses rather than an explicit warning pointing at\nthe cause.\n\n### Laying out nodes and edges: the body of `concept_map()`\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-node-layout\n#| eval: false\nnodes \u003c- do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(k), function(ci) {\n  labels \u003c- columns[[ci]]\n  m \u003c- length(labels)\n  data.frame(\n    col = ci, idx = seq_len(m),\n    x = (ci - 1) * dx,\n    y = (m - 1) / 2 * dy - (seq_len(m) - 1) * dy,\n    label = vapply(labels, wrap_label, character(1), width = wrap_width[ci]),\n    fill = fill_colors[ci],\n    rx = rx[ci], ry = ry[ci],\n    stringsAsFactors = FALSE\n  )\n}))\n```\n\nEach column is laid out **independently**: column `ci` sits at\n`x = (ci - 1) * dx`, and its own `m` items are centred vertically around\n`y = 0` (the formula places item 1 at `+((m-1)/2)*dy` and the last item at\nthe mirror-image negative value). A consequence worth knowing: columns\nwith different numbers of items are *each* centred on `y = 0`\nindependently, so a specific row position doesn't correspond to the same\nitem across columns — a 3-item column and a 2-item column don't have their\nfirst items aligned. This matches the McElreath-style figure (where\ncorrespondence is carried by the edges, not by row alignment) but is worth\nchecking visually for your own content.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-edge-endpoints\n#| eval: false\nedge_df \u003c- do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(k - 1), function(ci) {\n  e \u003c- edges[[ci]]\n  do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(nrow(e)), function(j) {\n    p1 \u003c- node_xy(ci, e$from[j])\n    p2 \u003c- node_xy(ci + 1, e$to[j])\n    data.frame(x = p1[\"x\"] + p1[\"rx\"], y = p1[\"y\"],\n               xend = p2[\"x\"] - p2[\"rx\"], yend = p2[\"y\"])\n  }))\n}))\n```\n\nEdges run from the **right edge** of the source ellipse to the **left\nedge** of the target ellipse (`+rx`/`-rx` from each node's own centre, not\na shared/average `rx`), so differently-sized ellipses across columns still\nmeet their edges at their actual boundaries rather than at a\none-size-fits-all offset.\n\n### Drawing: arrows vs. plain correspondence lines\n\n```{r}\n#| label: show-arrows-param\n#| eval: false\ngeom_curve(data = edge_df, aes(x = x, y = y, xend = xend, yend = yend),\n           curvature = curvature, color = edge_color, alpha = edge_alpha,\n           linewidth = 0.6,\n           arrow = if (arrows) arrow(length = unit(0.12, \"cm\"), type = \"closed\") else NULL)\n```\n\n`arrows = TRUE` (the default) draws a small arrowhead at each curve's\ntarget end, implying directionality. For a diagram about *correspondence*\nbetween theories/processes/statistical tests — not a causal chain — the\ndeck sets `arrows = FALSE`, giving plain connecting curves closer to\nMcElreath's original figure.\n\n```{r}\n#| label: demo-concept-map\n#| fig-cap: \"A small concept map, built directly from the script\"\n#| fig-width: 8\n#| fig-height: 4\ndemo_columns \u003c- list(\n  c(\"Theory A\", \"Theory B\"),\n  c(\"Process 1\", \"Process 2\"),\n  c(\"Test result X\")\n)\ndemo_edges \u003c- list(\n  data.frame(from = c(1, 1, 2), to = c(1, 2, 2)),\n  data.frame(from = c(1, 2), to = c(1, 1))\n)\nconcept_map(demo_columns, demo_edges, titles = c(\"Theories\", \"Processes\", \"Tests\"),\n            arrows = FALSE, curvature = 0)$plot\n```\n\n## Quarto usage / integration\n\nSetup-chunk line:\n\n```r\nsource(\"concept-map.R\")\n```\n\nReal usage from `doing-being-science.qmd` — the theory/process/statistical\ntest figure, with per-column structure built directly in the chunk\n(`theory_columns`, `theory_edges`, `theory_titles`) and plain (arrowless)\ncurves:\n\n````{verbatim}\n```{r}\n#| label: fig-theory-process-stats\n#| fig-cap: \"Different theories can imply the same process, and different processes can imply the same statistical test result\"\n#| fig-width: 9\n#| fig-height: 5.0625\n\ntheory_columns \u003c- list(\n  c(\"Words we experience more often are easier to recall\",\n    \"Frequency or age of experience does not matter to recall\",\n    \"Words we experience early in life are easier to recall\"),\n  c(\"Neural network connection strength changes\",\n    \"Neural word representation quality changes\",\n    \"Word recognition decision-making strategy changes\"),\n  c(\"Experience correlates with word recognition speed\",\n    \"Experience does not correlate with word recognition speed\")\n)\ntheory_titles \u003c- c(\"Theories\", \"Process models\", \"Statistical tests\")\n\n# theories -> processes: many-to-many\n# processes -> statistical models: every process links to both tests\ntheory_edges \u003c- list(\n  data.frame(from = c(1, 1, 2, 3, 3), to = c(1, 2, 3, 1, 2)),\n  data.frame(from = rep(1:3, each = 2), to = rep(1:2, times = 3))\n)\n\nconcept_map(theory_columns, theory_edges, theory_titles, arrows = FALSE, curvature = 0,\n            edge_color = \"#B0B0B0\", edge_alpha = 0.45)$plot\n```\n````\n\nNotice the edges are **generated** rather than hand-enumerated where a\npattern exists (`rep(1:3, each = 2)`/`rep(1:2, times = 3)` for \"every\nprocess links to both tests\") — a useful pattern when a column's edges\nare systematic rather than ad hoc.\n\n### Parameter quick reference\n\n| Parameter | Default | Meaning |\n|---|---|---|\n| `columns` | — | List of character vectors; node labels per column. |\n| `edges` | — | List (length `length(columns)-1`); each a data frame of 1-based `from`/`to` indices. |\n| `titles` | `NULL` | Optional column headings. |\n| `rx`, `ry` | `1.15, 0.62` | Ellipse half-width/height; single value or one per column. |\n| `dx`, `dy` | `3.3, 1.55` | Column spacing / within-column item spacing. |\n| `wrap_width` | `16` | Label wrap width (characters); single value or one per column. |\n| `fill_colors` | 5 pastel hexes | One per column; extended via `colorRampPalette()` if too few given. |\n| `edge_color`, `edge_alpha` | `\"#888888\"`, `0.7` | Curve styling. |\n| `curvature` | `0.2` | Passed to `geom_curve()`; `0` = straight lines. |\n| `arrows` | `TRUE` | `FALSE` for plain correspondence lines (no arrowheads). |\n\n## Exercises\n\n1. Add a fourth theory sentence to `theory_columns[[1]]` and a matching\n   row (or rows) to `theory_edges[[1]]`, pointing it at one or more\n   existing process models. What happens if you forget to add the edge?\n2. Deliberately set one `edges[[...]]$from` value to `99` (out of range)\n   and re-render. Read the resulting error message — does it tell you\n   exactly which column and index is wrong?\n3. Flip `arrows` to `TRUE` and `curvature` to `0.3` on the\n   theory/process/stats figure. Discuss: does the figure now seem to\n   claim something it shouldn't (causality, rather than\n   correspondence)?\n4. Give the \"Theories\" column a larger `rx`/`ry` and a wider\n   `wrap_width` (e.g. `rx = 1.6, ry = 0.9, wrap_width = 22`) while\n   leaving the other columns at their defaults, using the per-column\n   vector form described above. Confirm the long sentences fit inside\n   their ellipses afterward.\n5. Build a two-column (not three) concept map for a different topic you\n   teach, with at least one many-to-many edge, from scratch.\n\n## See also\n\nThis script has no dependencies on, and no dependents among, the other\nfour scripts in this set — it's used independently for the\ntheory/process/statistical-test slide, thematically preceding the\nsampling/measurement-error sequence built from\n[testing-sequence-walkthrough.qmd](testing-sequence-walkthrough.qmd) and\n[sampling-distributions-walkthrough.qmd](sampling-distributions-walkthrough.qmd).\n"},"elapsedMs":7}</script></tool-result></div>
